Q1
Answer briefly:   [20]
CO-2;   a.   Define Scenario-Based Design and explain how scenarios help in [5]
BL-2   designing interactive systems.
Definition of Scenario-Based Design (2 Marks)
Scenario-Based Design (SBD) is a human-computer interaction (HCI)
approach where designers create concrete, narrative stories (scenarios)
about how a specific persona will use a future system to achieve a goal.
Instead of starting with technical specifications, SBD starts with a
realistic situation—detailing the user's motivations, environment, and
task flow before any code is written or screens are designed.
How Scenarios Help in Designing Interactive Systems (3 Marks)
• Provides Context and Empathy: Scenarios ground the design in
reality. They force designers to consider environmental factors
(e.g., "The user is using the app outdoors in bright sunlight with
one hand") and emotional states (e.g., "The user is in a rush to
catch a train"), ensuring the interface accommodates these real-
world constraints.
• Improves Stakeholder Communication: Because scenarios are
written in plain, narrative language (like a short story) rather than
technical jargon, they make it incredibly easy for developers,
business managers, and clients to align on what the system should
actually do.
• Guides Testing and Requirement Gathering: Scenarios act as a
benchmark throughout the development lifecycle. They help
extract exact system requirements early on and provide ready-
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
made, realistic test cases for usability testing later (e.g., "Can the
user successfully complete the scenario we wrote in phase one?").
b.   A user experiences discomfort while using a wearable device for long
CO-2;   durations. Explain how ergonomics plays a role in improving user   [5]
BL-2
comfort and usability.
Ergonomics in System Design (1 Mark): Ergonomics is the study of the
physical characteristics of interaction. It is the process of designing
workplaces and systems to fit the people who use them by defining strict
standards and guidelines for how interfaces should be built.
1. Arrangement of Controls and Displays (1 Mark): Interface
controls must be logically grouped according to their specific
function, frequency of use, or sequential order to match human
workflow and physical reach.
2. Surrounding Environment (1 Mark): The physical workspace
must be adaptable. For example, providing seating arrangements that
can easily cope with all sizes of users prevents musculoskeletal pain
over long durations.
3. Health Issues (1 Mark): System design must account for the user's
physical position and optimize environmental conditions like
temperature, humidity, lighting, and noise to prevent fatigue.
4. Use of Colour (1 Mark): Designers should use established colour
meanings (e.g., red for a warning, green for okay) while maintaining
a strict awareness of colour-blindness so the interface remains
universally accessible.
By applying ergonomics, wearable devices become comfortable, safe,
and intuitive, allowing users to wear and interact with them for longer
durations without fatigue or discomfort, ultimately improving usability
and satisfaction.
c.   Apply the GOMS model to the task of “Uploading a photo to a social
CO-2;   media app”. Identify Goals, Operators, Methods, and Selection rules, and [5]
BL-3
provide a step-by-step script of user actions.
GOMS Model Analysis: Uploading a Photo to a Social Media App
1. Goals (G) (1 Mark)
• G1: Open the social media app
• G2: Navigate to the photo upload section
• G3: Select a photo to upload
• G4: Add caption or description (optional)
• G5: Apply filters or edits (optional)
• G6: Upload/post the photo
2. Operators (O) (1 Mark)
• Tap icon/button
• Swipe or scroll
• Type text
•   Select from gallery
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
• Confirm or cancel
• Apply filter/adjust slider
3. Methods (M) (1 Mark)
Method 1: Uploading a photo from the device gallery
1. Tap the social media app icon to open it
2. Tap the “+” or “Upload” button
3. Select “Photo/Video” option
4. Browse device gallery
5. Tap desired photo
6. (Optional) Add caption or description
7. (Optional) Apply filter or edit
8. Tap “Post” or “Upload” button
Method 2: Uploading a photo by taking a new picture
1. Tap the social media app icon
2. Tap “+” or “Upload”
3. Select “Camera” option
4. Capture photo
5. Confirm the photo
6. (Optional) Add caption or edit
7. Tap “Post/Upload”
4. Selection Rules (S) (1 Mark)
• S1: If the user already has a photo in the gallery → follow
Method 1
• S2: If the user wants to take a new photo → follow Method 2
• S3: If caption is optional → skip step if not needed
• S4: If editing is optional → skip step if not needed
Step-by-Step User Action Script (Example for Gallery Upload) (1
Mark)
1. Unlock device
2. Tap social media app icon
3. Tap “+” or “Upload” button
4. Choose “Photo/Video”
5. Browse gallery and tap desired photo
6. (Optional) Type caption in text field
7. (Optional) Apply filters or edit photo
8. Tap “Post/Upload” button
9. Wait for confirmation that the upload is complete
d.   A healthcare company wants to develop a voice-based assistant for
CO-4;   elderly users to help them manage daily tasks such as medication [5]
BL-6   reminders, emergency calls, and health queries. Design a voice-based
user interface system for this scenario.
Problem & Objective (1 Mark), Design Considerations (1 Mark),
Core Features/Voice Commands (1 Mark), Interaction Flow (1
Mark), Design Principles & Optional Enhancements (1 Mark).
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
A voice-based assistant can help elderly users manage tasks like
medication reminders, emergency calls, and health queries. The system
should use simple, natural language commands, provide clear feedback
and confirmations, and allow easy error recovery. Core features include:
setting reminders (“Remind me to take my medicine at 8 AM”), making
emergency calls (“Call my emergency contact”), and answering health-
related queries (“What is my next doctor appointment?”). The interaction
flow involves the user speaking a command, the system recognizing it,
confirming the action, executing the task, and providing feedback.
Design principles applied include user-centered design, recognition over
recall, error prevention, and clear feedback to ensure comfort, usability,
and safety. Optional enhancements may include adjustable voice speed
and integration with wearable devices.
Q2
a.   A fitness app is being redesigned. Analyze which design approach —
CO-2;   User-Centered, Participatory, or Scenario-Based, will best capture user [10]
BL-4
needs.
Redesigning a fitness app requires understanding a highly diverse user
base with varying goals (weight loss, muscle gain, marathon training) and
contexts (at the gym, running outdoors, quick home workouts). Capturing
these needs accurately is the difference between an app users open daily
and one they delete after a week.
1. User-Centered Design (UCD) (3 Marks)
• How it works: This approach places the user at the center of the
entire development process. It relies heavily on research (surveys,
interviews, analytics from the old app version), creating user
personas, and iterative usability testing.
•   Fitness App Application: Designers would look at data to see
where users drop off in the current app. They would interview
"Weekend Warriors" or "Daily Lifters" to understand their pain
points, design a prototype, and watch them try to use it.
• Pros: Highly data-driven and iterative. It ensures the final product
is highly usable and validates assumptions before development.
• Cons: Users are treated as subjects to be studied rather than active
creators. It might capture what users do, but sometimes misses the
deeper emotional why behind their fitness struggles.
2. Participatory Design (3 Marks)
• How it works: Also known as co-design, this approach turns
users into active members of the design team. Instead of just
interviewing users, designers run workshops where users sketch
out their ideal interfaces or features.
•   Fitness App Application: Inviting personal trainers, gym
novices, and mobility-impaired users to a workshop. They might
use paper prototypes to build their "dream workout logger" or sort
feature cards by priority.
• Pros: Exceptional for capturing deep, unspoken, or emotional
user needs. Fitness is deeply personal; having users co-create
ensures the app addresses real-world motivations and insecurities.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
• Cons: It can be chaotic, time-consuming, and resource-heavy.
Users know what they want, but they aren't UI/UX experts, so
their literal designs often need heavy translation into functional
software.
3. Scenario-Based Design (3 Marks)
• How it works: This method focuses on creating specific, narrative
use cases (scenarios) and designing interfaces to support those
exact situations.
•   Fitness App Application: Designing specifically for scenarios
like: "Sarah is running outdoors in the rain and needs to pause
her workout with sweaty hands," or "John is in a crowded gym
with a weak internet connection and needs to pull up his offline
workout routine."
• Pros: Brilliant for capturing contextual and environmental needs.
Fitness apps are often used in extreme physical conditions
(motion, glare, sweat, fatigue), and this approach ensures the
interface survives the real world.
• Cons: It can be too narrow. If designers fail to imagine a specific
scenario, those user needs are entirely missed. It focuses more on
the context of use than the overarching user journey.
While Participatory Design is incredible for brainstorming and Scenario-
Based Design is essential for physical context, User-Centered Design is the
best holistic approach for capturing user needs during a redesign [1 Mark].
Because it is a redesign, you already have existing users and usage data.
UCD allows you to anchor the project in hard analytics while systematically
testing new features. However, the most successful strategy would be a UCD
framework that borrows from the other two—using participatory workshops
in the early research phase and scenario-testing during the prototype phase.
b.   An online shopping website is facing issues such as inconsistent design,
CO-2;   lack of feedback, and frequent user errors during checkout. Design an
BL-6   improved user interface using HCI design rules and Shneiderman’s 8 [10]
golden rules. Your answer should include appropriate design solutions
for usability and user satisfaction.
To effectively resolve the shopping website's specific issues—
inconsistent design, poor feedback, and frequent checkout errors—we
must systematically apply Shneiderman’s 8 Golden Rules of Interface
Design. This framework targets the friction points to reduce cognitive
load, improve usability, and ensure a seamless purchasing journey.
1. Fixing Inconsistent Design (2.5 Marks)
Inconsistency forces users to relearn the interface on every page, causing
frustration and abandonment.
• Rule 1. Strive for consistency: Create a unified, site-wide design
system. Ensure primary calls-to-action (like "Add to Cart") use
the exact same color, shape, and typography everywhere.
Standardize terminology (e.g., exclusively use "Cart" instead of
mixing it with "Basket") and keep layout grids identical from the
homepage to the payment screen.
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
• Rule 8. Reduce short-term memory load: Users should not have
to remember their selections. Implement a persistent, slide-out
mini-cart accessible on every page. During checkout, constantly
display a detailed order summary (items, prices, taxes) on the side
of the screen so the user doesn't have to navigate backward to
verify their order.
2. Solving the Lack of Feedback (2.5 Marks)
Without system feedback, users feel lost, click multiple times, and doubt
if the site is working.
• Rule 3. Offer informative feedback: Every user action requires
an immediate response. When a user clicks "Add to Cart," the
button should briefly change to "Added ✓," alongside a small
animation that updates the cart's item count. During checkout,
utilize clear loading spinners on the "Pay" button so users know
their payment is processing.
• Rule 4. Design dialogs to yield closure: The purchasing journey
needs a definitive, reassuring end. After payment, automatically
route the user to a dedicated "Order Confirmed" page that
explicitly states the transaction was successful, displays an order
number, provides delivery estimates, and confirms an email
receipt was sent.
3. Eliminating Checkout Errors (2.5 Marks)
Checkout is the most critical phase; high error rates directly translate to
lost sales.
• Rule 5. Offer error prevention and simple error handling:
Stop errors before the user clicks submit. Use real-time inline
validation: show a green checkmark as a user correctly formats
their email, or a red highlight with helpful text (e.g., "Please
include an '@' symbol") the moment they make a mistake. Auto-
format credit card numbers with spaces to prevent typos.
• Rule 6. Permit easy reversal of actions: Ensure users feel safe
making edits. Provide obvious "Remove" buttons and quantity
adjusters in the cart. If a user deletes an item, offer a brief "Undo"
notification. Crucially, ensure that hitting the "Back" button
during a multi-step checkout does not erase the shipping or
payment data they have already entered.
4. Enhancing User Control & Efficiency (2.5 Marks)
Once the main friction points are removed, the site must be optimized for
user autonomy and speed.
• Rule 7. Support internal locus of control: Users should feel they
are driving the interface, not being forced by it. Avoid unexpected
pop-ups that disrupt their task. Give them the freedom to complete
their purchase using a "Guest Checkout" option rather than
trapping them behind a mandatory account registration wall.
• Rule 2. Enable frequent users to use shortcuts: Returning
customers need speed to bypass the standard flow. Implement
features like "Save this card for future purchases," a checkbox to
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
"Use shipping address as billing address," and one-click
reordering for past purchases to dramatically accelerate their
checkout process.
Q3
a.   Explain the goal of HCI and why understanding user behavior is
CO-1;   important in interface design. Provide examples of good and bad interface [10]
BL-2
designs.
The Goal of HCI (2 Marks)
The primary goal of Human-Computer Interaction is to create digital
systems that are effective, efficient, safe, and satisfying for people to use.
It ensures that technology adapts to human needs and capabilities, rather
than forcing humans to adapt to complex machines.
Importance of Understanding User Behavior (4 Marks)
Designers must understand how users actually behave—not how they
should behave—to build functional interfaces.
• Reduces Cognitive Load: Knowing humans have limited short-
term memory ensures designers keep critical information visible
rather than forcing users to memorize it.
• Matches Mental Models: Interfaces must align with users' past
experiences (e.g., expecting a magnifying glass icon to mean
"Search") to prevent confusion.
• Designs for Scanning: Because users scan pages instead of
reading them, understanding this behavior dictates the use of clear
visual hierarchy, bullet points, and prominent buttons.
• Anticipates Errors: Humans inevitably make mistakes.
Understanding this allows designers to build forgiving systems
(like "Undo" functions and clear error messages) rather than
punishing the user.
Examples of Good Interface Design (2 Marks)
• Streamlined E-commerce Checkout: A clear visual progress bar
(Cart -> Shipping -> Payment) that tells users exactly where they
are, combined with a quick "Guest Checkout" option to reduce
friction.
• Context-Aware Fitness Apps: Large, high-contrast text and
oversized "Stop/Pause" buttons designed specifically to be easily
tapped by a moving, sweating user.
Examples of Bad Interface Design (2 Marks)
• Misleading Visual Cues ("Norman Doors"): A destructive
action like "Delete Account" styled as a bright, inviting green
button, while the safe "Cancel" button is a muted, easily ignored
grey.
• Unforgiving Web Forms: A long registration page that
completely erases all user-entered data when submitted with a
single error, failing to highlight exactly which field was wrong.
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
CO-3;   b.   A learning management system has a complex course interface. Apply
BL-3   UX elements to redesign the course page so that students can access [10]
lessons, assignments, and progress reports easily.
The current LMS interface is complex due to:
•   Difficult navigation between lessons and assignments
• Poor visibility of progress reports
• Cluttered layout causing confusion.
1. Strategy Plane (2 Marks)
• Objective: Reduce cognitive overload so students focus on
learning rather than navigating.
• Redesign Action: Shift the platform's core strategy from being a
complex "file storage" system to an active, user-friendly task
manager.
2. Scope Plane (2 Marks)
• Objective: Define exact functional and content requirements.
• Redesign Action: Strip away clutter and limit the core features to
three   essentials: Lesson Delivery   (content), Assignment
Management (submissions), and Progress Tracking (grades).
3. Structure Plane (2 Marks)
• Objective: Organize the Information Architecture (IA) and
interaction design.
• Redesign Action: Replace deeply nested folders with a flat
hierarchy, categorizing all course material into three main pillars:
Lessons, Assignments, and Progress.
4. Skeleton Plane (2 Marks)
• Objective: Design the screen layout and navigation systems
(Wireframing).
• Redesign Action: Implement a persistent left-hand navigation
sidebar, top-page breadcrumb trails (e.g., Home > Week 3), and a
central "Up Next" widget for pending tasks.
5. Surface Plane (2 Marks)
• Objective: Apply visual design, typography, and sensory cues.
• Redesign Action: Add a visual course progress bar at the top, use
color-coded icons (like bold green checkmarks for finished work),
and use high-contrast text for urgent due dates.
Q4
CO-2;   a.   Create a Hierarchical Task Analysis (HTA) for “Making a cup of tea”.
BL-6   Detail all the main tasks and subtasks, showing the hierarchy and [10]
sequence clearly.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
[7] Marks for HTA diagrammatically / textually representation and [3]
marks for plan.
Hierarchical task analysis (HTA) is typical of such an approach. The
outputs of HTA are a hierarchy of tasks and subtasks and also plans
describing in what order and under what conditions subtasks are performed.
The task hierarchy can be represented diagrammatically as well as textually.
Below figure shows a task hierarchy for making a cup of tea. The main task,
‘make a cup of tea’, is decomposed into six subtasks. Of these only the first,
‘boil water’, is expanded further. The remaining tasks 2–6 and the subtasks
of 1.1–1.4 are underlined showing that the analysis has been deliberately
stopped at that point. This obviously denotes the same information as the
textual form, but may be more accessible at a glance.
Having produced a first stab at a task hierarchy, one would examine it for
errors or omissions. One way of approaching this would be to describe the
steps in the task hierarchy to a domain expert. This would quickly show that
the plan for making tea has a significant error – it forgets to warm the pot.
This has to be added between tasks 2 and 3. It is the nature of expert
knowledge that obvious things get missed for a task description. One way
the analyst can search for such omissions is by examining the form of the
subtasks. For example, 1.4 says ‘turn off gas’, but nowhere does it say to
turn the gas on! Probably, this was implicit in ‘put kettle on hob’, but it
should be added between tasks 1.2 and 1.3. At this point we might notice
that the task hierarchy is a little unbalanced. This might be right, but we may
have included too many detailed tasks at the highest level. We choose to add
a new top-level node ‘make pot’ which would encompass the tasks 3 and 4
and also the new ‘warm pot’ task.
b.   A software development team collects quantitative data such as task
CO-2;   completion time, number of errors, and user success rate while testing an [10]
BL-4   application. Analyze how this quantitative data can be used to improve
the interface design.
Quantitative data provides objective, measurable evidence of usability
issues. While qualitative data (like user interviews) explains why a user
is struggling, quantitative data pinpoints exactly where and how often the
struggles occur, removing subjective bias from design decisions
[0.5 Mark].
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
1. Utilizing Task Completion Time 3 Marks)
• What it indicates: Also known as "Time on Task," a high
completion time   suggests   cognitive   overload, confusing
navigation, or an inefficient layout that forces users to hunt for
information.
• Design Improvement: Designers can use this data to streamline
workflows. If a checkout process takes too long, the team can
apply Fitts’s Law (making primary buttons larger and closer to the
user's cursor/thumb), flatten the information architecture, or
introduce shortcuts like auto-fill forms to accelerate the process.
2. Utilizing Number of Errors (3 Marks)
• What it indicates: A high error rate (e.g., clicking the wrong
button, entering invalid data) highlights a lack of intuitive design,
poor affordances (buttons that don't look clickable), or confusing
terminology.
• Design Improvement: This data signals a need for better error
prevention and recovery mechanisms. Designers can improve the
interface by adding real-time inline validation to forms, rewriting
ambiguous button labels, increasing the contrast of critical
actions, or implementing "Undo" functionalities.
3. Utilizing User Success Rate (3 Marks)
• What it indicates: This is the most critical metric. A low success
rate (e.g., users failing to complete a registration or find a specific
setting) means the interface has fundamental usability blockers or
broken user journeys.
• Design Improvement: If users consistently abandon a task
halfway through, designers must completely redesign that specific
user flow. Solutions include breaking complex, multi-page tasks
into bite-sized steps with clear progress bars, or making the
primary Call to Action (CTA) significantly more prominent.
Quantitative data acts as a diagnostic tool. Once the development team
identifies the specific bottlenecks using these three metrics, they can
formulate a hypothesis, redesign the problematic interface elements, and
run A/B tests to see if the new metrics improve. This creates a continuous,
data-driven cycle of UI/UX enhancement [0.5 Mark].
Q5
CO-2;   a.   Design an interaction flow for an e-commerce checkout process, showing [10]
BL-6   steps from adding items to completing payment.
The E-commerce Checkout Interaction Flow
The primary goal of this interaction flow is to guide the user seamlessly
from product selection to final payment while providing continuous
feedback and maintaining a low cognitive load.
********** 10 **********
--- page 11 ---
Phase 1: Initiation & Cart Addition (2 Marks)
• User Action: The user clicks the primary "Add to Cart" button on
the product page.
• System Response (Feedback): The system provides immediate,
non-disruptive feedback. The button temporarily changes to
"Added ✓". A slide-out "mini-cart" appears on the right side of
the screen for 3 seconds, showing the added item, subtotal, and
two distinct options: "View Cart" or "Proceed to Checkout".
Phase 2: Cart Review (2 Marks)
• User Action: The user clicks "Proceed to Checkout" from the
mini-cart or the main navigation icon.
• System Response (Interface): The system displays the full cart.
o Interaction Details: Users can easily edit quantities or
remove items (triggering a quick "Undo" option). A
persistent "Order Summary" panel appears on the right
side of the screen, detailing item costs, estimated taxes,
and shipping thresholds. The user clicks a prominent, high-
contrast "Checkout" button.
Phase 3: Authentication & Shipping (2 Marks)
• User Action: The user encounters the authentication gateway and
selects their path.
• System Response (Interface): * Authentication: The system
offers three clear paths: "Log In," "Create Account," or "Guest
Checkout." (Forcing account creation is avoided to prevent drop-
offs).
o Shipping Entry: Once the path is chosen, the system
displays the shipping form. It uses real-time inline
validation (green checkmarks for correct formatting, red
text for errors) as the user types. An option to "Use
Shipping Address for Billing" is checked by default.
Phase 4: Payment & Final Review (2 Marks)
• User Action: The user selects a shipping speed and enters
payment details.
• System Response (Interface):
o Payment Entry: The system provides options (Credit Card,
PayPal, Google/Apple Pay). Credit card fields auto-format
with spaces and automatically detect the card type (Visa,
Mastercard) based on the first few digits.
o Final Review: Before the final click, the system presents
one last summary showing the exact final charge, shipping
address, and delivery date. The final Call to Action (CTA)
button clearly states "Place Order - $XX.XX".
Phase 5: Order Confirmation & Closure (2 Marks)
• User Action: The user clicks "Place Order".
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
• System Response (Feedback): The button transforms into a
loading spinner briefly. The system then transitions to a clean
"Thank You" page. This page displays the confirmation number,
an estimated delivery timeline, and a message stating that an email
receipt has been sent, providing the user with complete
psychological closure.
A well-designed interaction flow reduces user effort, errors, and
abandonment rate, leading to a smooth and satisfying shopping
experience.
b.   A dashboard used by manager’s displays large amounts of data, making
CO-2;   it difficult to interpret and remember key information. Apply principles [10]
BL-3   of visual perception and memory to improve this interface. Explain how
each principle helps enhance usability.
Minimizing Cognitive Burden (2 Marks)
A data-heavy dashboard overwhelms a manager's working memory. By
applying principles heavily influenced by George Miller and Don
Norman, along with Gestalt psychology, the interface can minimize
cognitive burden and shift the processing effort from the user's mind to
the screen.
1. Visual Perception & Organization (4 Marks)
• Key Gestalt Principles (e.g., Proximity): Group related data
points physically close together (e.g., clustering all financial
metrics in one visual "card"). This helps the user automatically
perceive structure and relationships without mental effort.
• Visual Hierarchy: Use size, color, and contrast (e.g., a massive
red arrow for dropping sales) to guide attention. This reduces
"preattentive" strain, allowing the manager to process critical
anomalies efficiently.
• Consistency: Use consistent design, terminology, and visual
patterns across all dashboard widgets. This helps the manager
apply existing long-term memory (LTM) to new situations,
speeding up interpretation.
2. Memory & Cognitive Load Principles (4Marks)
• Chunking: Group massive tables of data into manageable units
(e.g., 4 high-level regional summaries) to fit within the strict
limits of short-term memory (STM).
• Recognition over Recall: Replace raw data tables with visual
graphs (like bar charts or sparklines). It is much easier for a
manager to recognize a visual trend than to recall and calculate
previous numbers from memory.
• Reduce Cognitive Load & "Mode" Errors: Never force the
manager to remember information from one tab or screen to
another; keep all required context visible. Additionally, minimize
systems with different "modes" where the same action yields
different results, as this heavily strains working memory.
• Support "Closure": Ensure data analysis tasks (like exporting a
report or applying a complex filter) have clear, logical endings,
********** 12 **********
--- page 13 ---
allowing the manager to move to the next task while giving their
memory a break.
Q6
CO-3;   a.   A hotel booking site has a messy homepage. Revamp the UI to make
BL-3   important information and actions stand out, using visual hierarchy, [10]
spacing, iconography, and color to guide users effectively.
The current hotel booking homepage is messy and cluttered, making it
hard for users to find essential elements such as search, deals, or booking
options. Users may get overwhelmed with too much text, multiple
banners, inconsistent fonts, or poorly organized sections, leading to
confusion, slower task completion, and reduced bookings [1 Mark].
Applying UI Design Principles (8 Marks)
1. Visual Hierarchy
• Place the search bar and “Book Now” buttons prominently at the
top, with large fonts and contrasting colors.
•   Highlight critical sections like “Best Deals”, “Top Destinations”,
and “Featured Hotels” using bold headings or larger images.
• Use typographic hierarchy: headings > subheadings > body text.
Benefit: Users quickly identify and access key actions.
2. Spacing and Layout
• Separate content into clear sections with sufficient white space,
reducing clutter.
• Organize the page in a grid layout, with consistent alignment of
images, text, and buttons.
• Group related items, such as hotel offers or reviews, into cards or
panels for easy scanning.
Benefit: Reduces cognitive load and allows users to focus on one section
at a time.
3. Iconography
• Use icons to enhance recognition of actions and information:
o Calendar icon for date selection
o Location pin for destination
o Star icons for hotel ratings
o Bed icon for room types
• Combine icons with text labels for clarity.
Benefit: Improves scanning speed and helps users understand content
quickly.
4. Color Usage
• Apply contrasting colors for primary actions like “Search” or
“Book Now” to make them stand out.
********** 13 **********
--- page 14 ---
• Use softer or neutral background colors for secondary
information (offers, promotions) to reduce visual noise.
• Use color consistently: e.g., green for available bookings, red for
unavailable rooms.
Benefit: Directs attention and improves usability.
5. Feedback and Interaction
• Provide hover effects on buttons and cards to indicate
interactivity.
•   Include small animations or notifications when users select dates
or filter options.
Benefit: Confirms actions and makes the interface feel responsive.
6. Consistency
• Maintain uniform fonts, button styles, and spacing across the
homepage.
• Ensure alignment of images and text is consistent across sections.
Benefit: Users can predict interactions and navigate confidently.
As a result of the redesign, users can locate the search bar and booking
options instantly, and critical information such as deals, destinations, and
hotel ratings is easy to scan. The navigation becomes more intuitive,
reducing cognitive load and allowing users to complete tasks efficiently.
Overall usability, task efficiency, and user satisfaction are significantly
improved. Additionally, optional enhancements like filter options for
price, rating, and amenities near the search bar, recommendation
carousels for personalized suggestions, and mobile-responsive design
further support a seamless and engaging user experience [1 Mark].
b.   A company evaluates its interface only through user interviews and
CO-2;   feedback sessions, without using any quantitative or experimental [10]
BL-5   methods. Evaluate whether this approach is sufficient for effective
interface evaluation.
Relying strictly on user interviews and feedback sessions is insufficient
for a comprehensive interface evaluation. While qualitative methods are
essential, using them without quantitative or experimental data creates
massive blind spots in usability testing. Effective evaluation requires
"triangulation"—combining different methods to get a complete picture
[1 Mark].
1. The Strengths of the Current Approach (2 Marks)
• What it does well (The "Why"): Qualitative methods excel at
uncovering the reasons behind user behavior. Interviews allow
teams to explore users' mental models, emotional frustrations, and
subjective satisfaction.
********** 14 **********
--- page 15 ---
• Value: It is highly effective for gathering feature requests,
understanding brand perception, and discovering context-of-use
scenarios that designers might not have considered.
2. Limitation 1: The "Say vs. Do" Gap (2 Marks)
• The Problem: Human memory is flawed, and users are
notoriously bad at accurately reporting their own behavior.
• Impact on Evaluation: In feedback sessions, users often suffer
from "politeness bias" (telling interviewers what they think they
want to hear) or post-rationalization (making up logical reasons
for illogical clicks). A user might confidently say an interface was
"easy to use," completely forgetting that they clicked the wrong
button three times before succeeding.
3. Limitation 2: Missing Objective Metrics (2 Marks)
• The Problem: Interviews cannot accurately measure efficiency
or effectiveness, two core pillars of usability.
• Impact on Evaluation: Without quantitative tracking, the
company lacks hard data on Time on Task (how long a process
actually takes), Error Rates (how many mistakes are made per
session), and Task Success Rates (how many users actually
complete the goal vs. abandon it). You cannot optimize for speed
or accuracy using opinions alone.
4. Limitation 3: Lack of Statistical Significance (2 Marks)
• The Problem: Qualitative feedback is usually gathered from a
small sample size. A few vocal users might complain about a
specific feature, prompting the team to change it.
• Impact on Evaluation: Without experimental methods like A/B
testing on a larger scale, the company has no way to prove if a
redesign actually improves the experience for the majority of the
user base. They risk making sweeping design changes based on
anecdotal evidence.
To effectively evaluate an interface, a company must use a mixed-
methods approach (triangulation). Quantitative data tells you what is
happening and where the errors are occurring, while qualitative
interviews tell you why they are happening [1 Mark].
Q7
a.   A healthcare management system has multiple dashboards for doctors
and nurses. Users report inconsistent information display and difficulty
CO-2;   locating key data. Analyze the system using evaluation techniques in
BL-4   HCI.   [10]
• Compare heuristic evaluation and experimental evaluation models
for identifying issues.
• Recommend the most effective approach to improve usability.
[5] Marks for comparing evaluation models and [5] marks for
recommended approach.
********** 15 **********
--- page 16 ---
In a healthcare management system, usability flaws are not just
inconveniences; they are critical patient safety risks. The reported
issues—inconsistent displays and difficulty locating data—cause
cognitive overload for doctors and nurses who are already operating in
high-stress, time-sensitive environments. To fix this, we must evaluate
the system using established HCI methods.
1. Comparing Evaluation Models
To identify the root causes of these usability issues, the team can use two
distinct evaluation models.
A. Heuristic Evaluation (Expert-Based)
• How it works: UI/UX experts evaluate the dashboard against
established usability principles (like Nielsen’s 10 Heuristics).
They step through the interface looking for violations of
consistency, user control, and error prevention.
• Pros: It is fast, cost-effective, and does not require taking busy
doctors and nurses away from their patients. It is exceptionally
good at identifying surface-level UI problems (which directly
addresses the reported "inconsistent information display").
• Cons: The evaluators are design experts, not medical
professionals. They might flag a layout issue but completely
misunderstand the actual clinical workflow of a nurse.
B. Experimental Evaluation (User-Based/Usability Testing)
• How it works: Actual doctors and nurses are given specific,
realistic tasks (e.g., "Locate Patient A's allergy history and update
their medication") in a controlled environment. Researchers
collect quantitative data (time taken, error rate) and qualitative
data (vocalized frustrations).
• Pros: It reveals exactly how the interface performs under real
human constraints. It is the only definitive way to understand why
users are having "difficulty locating key data" during their actual
workflows.
• Cons: It is expensive, time-consuming, and logistically difficult
to schedule with active healthcare workers.
2. Recommended Approach: The Two-Phased Method
Relying on just one evaluation method is a mistake. The most effective
approach to improve the usability of this healthcare system is a hybrid,
two-phased strategy.
• Phase 1: Conduct a Heuristic Evaluation First
o Why: You should not waste a busy doctor's time testing an
interface that has obvious, easily fixable flaws. Have UX
experts comb through the system first to standardize
colors, fix broken links, align navigation menus, and
resolve the "inconsistent information display."
• Phase 2: Conduct Experimental Evaluation Second
o Why: Once the obvious visual inconsistencies are fixed,
bring in the actual doctors and nurses for task-based
testing. Give them simulated patient emergencies to see if
they can locate critical data quickly. If a nurse still
********** 16 **********
--- page 17 ---
struggles to find a patient's chart after the UI is cleaned up,
it indicates a deeper flaw in the system's Information
Architecture that must be redesigned.
By combining the speed of expert heuristics with the reality of
experimental user testing, the development team can resolve both the
surface-level inconsistencies and the deep, workflow-blocking issues.
CO-1;   b.   Discuss how multi-disciplinary approaches in HCI (e.g., psychology,
BL-2   ergonomics, design) contribute to creating user-friendly interfaces. [10]
Provide suitable example.
The Necessity of Multiple Disciplines (1 Marks)
Creating a truly user-friendly interface is impossible through computer
science alone. While engineering dictates how a system functions, HCI
relies on a multi-disciplinary approach to understand how a human
behaves. By synthesizing psychology, ergonomics, and design, HCI
bridges the gap between complex machine logic and human limitations.
1. The Role of Psychology (Cognitive Science) (3 Marks)
• Contribution: Psychology   provides   the framework   for
understanding how users perceive, process, learn, and remember
information. It ensures the interface does not overwhelm the user's
mental capacity (cognitive load).
• Application & Examples: * Mental Models: Designing a digital
"shopping cart" to behave like a physical one so users intuitively
know how to use it.
o Miller’s Law (Memory): Formatting a 10-digit phone
number as (123) 456-7890 (chunking) because short-term
memory struggles to process a raw string of 10 numbers.
o Gestalt Principles: Grouping related settings under a
single visually bordered section so the brain instantly
perceives them as connected.
2. The Role of Ergonomics (Human Factors) (3 Marks)
• Contribution: Ergonomics focuses on the physical interaction
between the human body and the device. It ensures that interfaces
are physically comfortable, accessible, and efficient to navigate
without causing strain.
• Application & Examples:
o   Fitts’s Law: Making primary Call-to-Action (CTA)
buttons larger and placing them closer to the user's natural
resting position (e.g., placing the "Checkout" button at the
bottom of a mobile screen where the thumb naturally
rests).
o Eye Strain & Accessibility: Implementing "Dark Mode"
to reduce ocular fatigue in low-light environments, or
ensuring high text contrast for visually impaired users.
o Touch Targets: Ensuring mobile buttons are at least
44x44 pixels so fingers can tap them accurately without
hitting adjacent links.
********** 17 **********
--- page 18 ---
3. The Role of Visual & Interaction Design (2 Marks)
• Contribution: Design transforms psychological and ergonomic
theories into a tangible, aesthetic interface. It uses visual
hierarchy to guide attention and establishes clear communication
between the system and the user.
• Application & Examples:
o Affordances & Signifiers: Making a button look 3D or
adding a drop shadow so it visually "invites" being clicked.
o Color Theory for Feedback: Universally using red to
signify destructive actions (like "Delete") and green to
signify   success   (like   "Payment   Approved"),
communicating status instantly without requiring users to
read text.
Multi-disciplinary approaches together create interfaces that are cognitively
efficient, physically comfortable, and visually intuitive.
Example: In healthcare systems, dashboards for doctors combine [1 Mark]:
• Psychology → highlight critical patient data using colors and alerts
• Ergonomics → arrange controls for quick reach and minimal clicks
• Design → layout panels and charts for easy scanning
********** 18 **********
````
